observation and product-deadline assertion.

Nested CLI and ordinary native Node baselines await owned command completion
asynchronously. Timeout reports `TEST_FIXTURE_COMMAND_TIMEOUT` distinctly and
releases the command's private group; detached file keepers also receive EOF
when the runner exits. Native verdicts, counts, failure text, shim execution
and inspection/keeper-error refusals remain assertions. Deliberately exercised
watchdog and compiler-deadline clocks remain distinct from these correctness
work allowances. A real hung-runner control must still fail and drain its
descendants; delayed-startup controls must preserve all original verdicts.
Initial Linux child capture can observe an empty cmdline during an interpreter
exec. It may wait up to two minutes for complete bytes, anchored to the first
live birth and the spawning parent. Changed births, malformed nonempty commands
and other read failures refuse. **FL round 9:** this wait yields through
abortable ten-millisecond timers. Spawn returns the owned child immediately;
the command watchdog is armed before capture and cleanup never awaits it.
Child exit/error cancels pending capture, including a native probe. Signal
APIs are awaitable: callers must await initial capture and the fresh exact
command/birth checks before dispatch. Identity checks themselves do not retry
incomplete observations. No blocking wait or synchronous subprocess runs in
the shared runner, signal or command helpers. Recorded-PID inspection is still
not atomic with the syscall; owned handles/groups remain preferred. The
[round-9 report](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-9/REPORT.md)
records heartbeat, timeout/reaping and anchor mutation controls.
Load verification must also keep its executable inode stable: rebuilding a
CLI whose live workers re-exec `current_exe()` invalidates that concurrent run.
See [FL round 8](../snapback4/reports/20261007-fl-dry-run-flakes/round-8/REPORT.md).

## 9. Architecture and code review, 2026-09-12

The author's request for a comprehensive review adds a findings record to
this root before opening focused research children. The review covers the
engine, compiler, HTTP/CLI host, both client tiers and stores, React arms,
reference apps, simulator, fixtures, witnesses and release tooling at
Snapback 4 tree `cf0a13dca32b3e1721d1d947a3f82c262a0807f0`
(repository commit `d6c803878a5432cce5f045777786a049972d3d70`, version
0.0.16). It does not change the bakeoff decisions or accept new designs.

The prescribed suite passes, but additional probes reproduce
schema-admission and projection failures, lost or regressed replica state,
incorrect predictions, stale observations, compiler semantic errors and
request-worker starvation. Passing the existing suite does not establish
the missing properties. The author's updated scope reserves security holes
for another model; this record covers architecture, correctness,
performance and ergonomics and makes no security-review claim.

Four questions need design work beyond an isolated patch:

- [LLP 3000.000](./3000.000-atomic-schema-and-release-activation.research.md):
  the atomic unit and owner of schema/release activation.
- [LLP 3000.001](./3000.001-durable-replica-and-write-lifecycle.research.md):
  confirmed replica data, speculative writes and durable outcomes.
- [LLP 3000.002](./3000.002-end-to-end-resource-bounds.research.md):
  physical resource bounds across requests and replication.
- [LLP 3000.003](./3000.003-one-semantics-and-credible-verification.research.md):
  one semantic contract across Rust, TypeScript and verification hosts.

The [detailed review and 37-ticket index](./reviews/3000-architecture-code-review-20260912.md)
record evidence, alternatives and acceptance examples. All four children are
Draft Research. They remain review findings and proposals; the core owner
retains implementation custody under §1 decision 4.

The author subsequently requested implementation of the 37 concrete tickets.
All 37 are now closed; the [correction qualification record](./reviews/3000-review-corrections-20260913.md)
contains the executed checks, independent reviews and remaining research scope.
Their resolution records and the children's `Implementation follow-up`
sections describe the delivered boundaries: transactional semantic activation,
confirmed-base replay and atomic snapshot ownership, bounded admission/output,
and compiler/client parity corrections. Integration preserves the concurrently
landed native Expo module and ordered-merge performance work. The four children
remain Draft Research for their explicitly outstanding choices; the ticket
fixes do not claim that every architecture question has been settled.

The separate [security review](./reviews/3000-security-review-20260913.md)
opened twelve findings across the HTTP, identity, storage, synchronization and
asset boundaries. Its
[remediation record](./reviews/3000-security-remediation-20260913.md) closes
those findings and three overlapping comprehensive-review findings. The four
security decisions in §7 state the lasting contract; individual tickets retain
the counterexamples, acceptance evidence and limits.

## How to orient

Read this document, then LLP 2000.000 for the argument and its evidence,
then `snapback4/AGENTS.md` once it exists, then the journal. For the
sibling's position read LLP 2000; for the incumbent, LLP 1000; for the
history both inherit, LLP 0329 and its family.

## Non-claims

- This document accepts nothing in LLP 2000.000 on the author's behalf; it
  is the brief, and only running code binds.
- It changes no Snapback 2 or Snapback 3 status, scope, ceiling or gate, and
  edits neither rules file.
- It decides no promotion; the comparison and its ruling are the author's.
- It claims no result; every number above is a threshold or a plan.

## 2026-09-27 — Global search beyond one read

Lane GS reproduced the global-search boundary and stopped at a design handoff.
The supervisor narrowed 0.4.6 to compact, bounded one-read membership search;
[LLP 3000.024](./3000.024-global-search-beyond-one-read.rfc.md) records the
Decision, implementation, measurements and explicit grouped-search refusal
beyond its envelope. The retained-session and stateless multi-pass contracts
remain future RFC work. Budgets, disclosure rules and the no-query-cache
exclusion are unchanged; round 1's report records qualification failures.
force_generic_plan, 36 cells × 3 repetitions; 50 warm-up + 300 measured
plain requests and 60 exact-handle EXPLAINs per timing mode per repetition.
No optimizer-statistics reset in that fixture. PostgreSQL 16.15, S1b's
8 GiB/300-connection/durable settings, measured 5 s statement/lock limits,
direct loopback only. Release builds used the owned target and `-j 8`.
Canonical load ranges (1/5/15 min) were 9.75–16.14 / 8.91–10.53 / 6.42–7.11;
no run was discarded. Fixture tables froze auto-analysis for the plan arms;
cluster autovacuum stayed on. Preliminary output-packing/EXPLAIN-wrapper
and reset-statistics diagnostics remain private, distinct from the final
natural-load/exact-handle matrix. No raw logs or credentials committed.

Validation: all cells, fact/oracle/order/absence checks, exact statement-call
and stable-byte witnesses, release build, rustfmt, Python compile, diff
check and ref-check (566 LLPs, 11,830 refs, zero errors). Raw custody is
`/tmp/claude-1000/-home-ccheever-projects-snapback/3d04fc0c-0698-42a7-917c-2880afdea35c/scratchpad/m1/`.
Postmaster 1540291 stopped; all 215 recorded owned PIDs absent;
`pg_ctl status`=3, no postmaster.pid or socket directory. The 25 GiB guard
held; post-stop free space was 48.97 GiB. No push, stash or Git config change.

## 2026-10-24 — M-suite: matched write attribution, flight witnesses, fill and reader pipelines

[Full results](./experiments/postgres-commit-path/results-20261024-msuite/README.md)
record M0/M2/M3/M1b under Accepted LLP 3000.009.001.000.002 §§3.4, 5–6,
10.1, 10.7 and 12. Measurements ran on expo-build-450b, with S1-PG4's private
PG16.15/durable settings, direct TCP, physical core split and A/B delay
proxies. Actual controller interval: 2026-10-23 21:44 through October 24
00:21 UTC. No product change or loaded engine-capacity claim.

M0 completes 260 cells/52 fit groups, three repetitions each, N=1/4/16/32/64,
fresh tiny-ANALYZE/reused backend, mature warm/new backend and forced generic,
one client versus max(4,2N) producers with durable per-member acknowledgements.
Warm producer e_total medians, full/no_ops/batch_tail/r_only, are
37.91/32.84/23.58/12.03 µs per member on A and
39.33/31.43/26.69/17.12 on B. Corresponding full-tail server marginals are
31.55/38.82 µs. All 160 warm/generic cells have zero ledger seq-scan/tuple
deltas in both intervals; all 60 contaminated cells are fresh RI arms and
remain labeled. Current main already removed the legacy FK, so these arms
restore it for attribution; ten current no-FK controls stay clean. Warm full
A's 125.02 ms worst transaction p99 and earlier 297/404 ms tails remain
visible. Per-run slopes, EXPLAIN/transaction/window components, WAL and
write/sync time are retained. Experimental packed history is only a cost bound.

The separate existing S1 engine arm checks 1,650 increments and measures
561.79 µs per mutation (median of run means) of calling-thread CPU around
Engine::mutate. Ingress session resolution is outside that timer; caller-side
driver/codec/meter work is inside, background I/O and SQL wait outside. It is
a solo-path CPU proxy, not incremental overlay interpreter CPU, and never
additional observations in the harness regression. Host captures enclose its
three repetitions together rather than individual repetition boundaries.

All 21 M2 witnesses pass: raising fence first, post-head-lock fresh generation
and receipt snapshots, zero-row count refusal, folded-flight aborts, deferred
COMMIT failure, loss/unknown and reconciliation. PG16.15 applies the timeout
per statement; bounded apply failure skips COMMIT. Explicit SET LOCAL
statement_timeout=0 permits the deliberately slow COMMIT; the no-reset
control also survives on this build and is disclosed. Equal-encoding simple
N32 cycles, three→two flights, are 8.639→7.653 ms on A and 9.868→8.889 ms
on B. The separately measured bound-apply three-flight comparator is
6.443/7.932 ms. Codec and cluster-epoch differences forbid a protocol-only
causal comparison. Retain three flights; two-flight product adoption still
owes the parent retry/round-trip amendments and wider unknown-outcome rule.

M3's 80 closed-loop simulator cells retain intended arrivals, C=32,
effective-N histograms, queue age and deadline failures. Saturation immediate
dispatch alternates N≈1/N≈31 nearly 100% of the time, effective N≈16;
250 µs collection reaches 27.44 and 1 ms reaches 32, removing alternation.
With the measured solo CPU proxy, only B's 400/s rung passes all three model
repetitions; A 2,262/3,200 and B 1,600 fail. Zero-incremental-CPU sensitivities
remain separate. No policy is accepted for product 3,200/s from this model.

M1b completes 16 cells × three repetitions of independent extended queries,
one Sync/query, depth 1/4/8/16 and NODELAY on/off. Native W2 facts, result
association, error isolation and independent snapshot witnesses pass. Split
frames/NODELAY off reproduce ~41/42 ms p50 A/B; coalescing or NODELAY removes
the stall locally. NODELAY-on depth-4 isolated reads imply optimistic
30-reader ceilings of 148,487/90,869 reads/s, versus depth-1 31,340/20,087;
this is not a loaded 30-reader/admission/SLO qualification. Depth 8/16 on all
30 readers also exceeds 128 external clients. The fixture is M1's 131,072
messages, not S1's 1M seed. 450a's loads 11.55 then 17.01 exclude the optional
cross-host arm; its q24 workload remains untouched.

RFC branches selected: current-layout G2/G3/bounded G3b and plan hygiene;
fill/CPU/service attribution before any A/F write migration; bounded reader
pipelining/admission before conditional G5/G6/D. Warm server work is below
the ≥100 µs/member commissioning trigger. Permanent A/F cancellation still
requires the composed product cycle and G3's real change-evidence insert/prune
tail confirmation. No G9 win follows from harness or model headroom.

The shared-host disk guard stopped epoch 1 at 29.993 GiB, with all owned PIDs
confirmed gone; space later fell to 4.768 GiB after shutdown. Only owned
private data/credentials/unused build cache were removed. After recovery
above 126 GiB and a quiet boundary, a fresh byte-identically configured
cluster completed epoch 2 (99/161 M0 cells per epoch). Thirty foreign-load
attempts, 17 idle-boundary failures, 33 earlier SQL-timing cells and the
closed first epoch remain in external custody. Final normal shutdown,
independent identity/path/port audits and credential cleanup pass; no owned
benchmark PID remains. The independent measurement audit and ref-check
(567 LLPs, 11,862 refs, zero errors) pass. Sources/raw evidence are archived
outside Git with hashes. No raw logs committed; no push, stash or Git config
change.

## Summary

Snapback is a complete backend for application projects. It is designed to
**scale to zero** — to be so light that an idle app costs almost nothing and
needs no babysitting, letting you prototype without worry — while being built so
the same system can **scale up** to the workload of any realistically popular
production app.

It is **comprehensive and coherent**: it aims to give you everything a backend
needs under one roof, with the pieces designed to work together rather than
bolted on, a bit like Ruby on Rails. And it is **truly open** — MIT licensed,
with no source-available restrictions that would stop you from hosting it for
other people.

This document is the root of the Snapback LLP corpus. It captures the project's
intent, its subsystem boundaries, and the non-obvious constraints that every
follow-on design and every line of code is expected to respect. It reflects the
founder's stated direction for the project as of the date above. Treat the
direction here as active; treat subsystem details as boundary-level guidance
until follow-on LLPs turn them into implementation specs.

The corpus is flat under `llp/`. `llp/foundation/` links the kernel documents
from which the design could be recreated; `llp/current/` links the declared
working set. A document keeps its path for its whole life — `Tombstoned` and
`Superseded` are header statuses, not directories. `reviews/`, `current/`, and
`foundation/` are reserved names. Orient from `foundation/`, then `current/`,
then the `@ref`s in scope.

## Source and ratification

This document is based on Charlie Cheever's Snapback project brief from
2026-06-25 and the same-day review/revision pass over the README and LLP corpus.
Charlie is the ratifying source for the product direction captured here. Future
LLPs should preserve this distinction: this root document states the current
direction, while subsystem LLPs define the specific contracts agents implement.

A cross-family review of this document (Claude / Anthropic) was recorded under
[`llp/reviews/0000-snapback.claude.md`](./reviews/0000-snapback.claude.md) on
2026-06-25. Editorial clarifications from it have been applied to sharpen wording
and surface gaps; they do not change the ratified product direction, which
remains Charlie's to set.

## Ratification ledger

This ledger records ratified decisions that cut across multiple LLPs, so the
corpus does not rely on scattered "pending Charlie" notes.

- **2026-06-25:** the root Snapback product direction in this document is
  Active.
- **2026-06-26:** the canonical first dev/test target is the full local server
  on Ibex + SQLite, as recorded in [LLP 0002](./0002-canonical-local-target.decision.md).
- **2026-06-26:** the first implementation slice is the narrow local proof
  captured in [LLP 0010](./0010-agent-dx-and-acceptance.spec.md) §V0
  Implementation Charter.
- **2026-06-26:** the first-slice execution/write boundary is accepted:
  poll-driven async handler completion, buffered `ctx.db`, snapshot reads,
  single-version commit, and commit-time read-set validation
  ([LLP 0003](./0003-local-code-hosting-and-storage-core.rfc.md) §Execution
  and Write Semantics; [LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md)
  §Runtime Boundary).
- **2026-06-26:** the first-slice app API decisions and v0 artifact schemas are
  accepted as implementation pins, with broader storage, sync, auth, and
  integration questions still Draft ([LLP 0004](./0004-application-data-api.rfc.md),
  [LLP 0012](./0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md)).
- **2026-06-26:** Cloudflare remains the planned second target, but only through
  a dedicated strategy and prototype, not as an assumed port of the local core
  ([LLP 0014](./0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md)).
- **2026-07-01:** the decision-and-implementation hardening program is accepted
  as the execution charter for the current phase, after a two-family review
  loop with independently verified registers; corpus status transitions,
  docket outcomes, and phase ratifications flow through its rules
  ([LLP 0032](./0032-decision-and-implementation-hardening.plan.md)).
- **2026-07-01:** the V1 implementation charter in
  [LLP 0010](./0010-agent-dx-and-acceptance.spec.md) is ratified, superseding
  the V0 charter: cron, actions with gated outbound fetch, ephemeral realtime,
  and the admin console are in-charter; the V1 must-not-claim list (snapshot
  reads until LLP 0032 Phase 2, reconnect/resume, packaged CLI, hosted
  execution, and the rest) stands as the honesty boundary.
- **2026-07-01:** license posture is decided — **MIT eyes-open** with named
  revisit triggers ([LLP 0033](./0033-license-posture.decision.md), docket
  DK-8): the free-riding exposure is a conscious trade for the ratified
  "truly open" value; a trademark policy is the compatible first response if
  a trigger fires.
- **2026-07-01:** read-set validation semantics (docket DK-1) are decided —
  **implement-up, staged** rather than ratifying the current table-granularity
  behavior as final ([LLP 0032](./0032-decision-and-implementation-hardening.plan.md)
  Phase 2, LLP 0003/0004/0007). Read-your-writes now works (E2); the remaining
  legs are host-plan read capture (E3 — a design question under the atomic
  single-transaction host-plan model, not a mechanical fix) and
  predicate-granularity validation (E4, gated on the M1 measurement). The
  execution-semantics docs are honest about what ships versus the target. (E4
  landed 2026-07-01 predicate-granular; E3 decided 2026-07-02 — Option A — see
  the 2026-07-02 entry below, which fully resolves DK-1.)
- **2026-07-01:** the server route taxonomy
  ([LLP 0031](./0031-server-route-taxonomy.spec.md)) → Active: LLP 0032 S1
  implemented the app-data/system boundary — ungated root `/admin/*` aliases
  retired for gated `/_snapback/api/*` twins, connection teardown and the
  ephemeral register route moved/retired, public `/inspect` trimmed, and a
  declared route registry with enforcement tests guards it (verified against a
  running server).
- **2026-07-01:** VM lifecycle (docket DK-4) is decided on the M1 evidence
  ([LLP 0035](./0035-local-target-benchmarks.research.md)): reducing
  per-invocation VM cost is the top compute priority — a warm VM per bundle
  hash for the **trusted-local** target only, gated on a proven per-invocation
  reset, with the cheapest safe step (decompose the cost; cache the
  per-invocation process-global host install) taken first. Hosted/untrusted
  keeps fresh-per-invocation; the five hosted-pooling proofs are unchanged
  ([LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md) §Invocation Isolation).
- **2026-07-01:** delta shape and read lane (docket DK-3) are decided
  deliberately on the M1 fan-out evidence: **whole-document `replace`, WS-only
  for v1**, because the fan-out cost is VM-bound (DK-4 is the higher-leverage
  fix) — with a named revisit trigger (warm-VM fan-out still exceeding target at
  a real subscriber count, or the hosted multi-subscriber target) and an
  explicit guardrail keeping the cacheable-HTTP-read-lane and op-log-delta
  options open in the wire protocol
  ([LLP 0008](./0008-local-first-sync-and-realtime.rfc.md) §Reactivity,
  [LLP 0012](./0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md)).
- **2026-07-01:** the LLP 0032 R1 status batch is ratified: LLP 0002 and the
  research documents LLP 0006/0019 → Active; LLP 0013, 0017, 0023, 0028,
  0031 → Accepted (each is the shipped design); LLP 0025 → Implemented. Two
  apply-time deviations, both honesty-preserving: LLP 0020 stays Draft
  (ratification-time verification found its §Remaining Phase 4 design work
  still open), and LLP 0001/0022 stay Draft (`ref-check` enforces that Active
  documents carry no `[inferred]` claims, and both keep inference-tagged
  analysis). All other documents remain Draft, verified accurate.
- **2026-07-02:** cron day-matching semantics (docket DK-6) are decided and
  ratified by Charlie: day-of-month and day-of-week combine with **POSIX OR**
  when both are restricted (`0 0 13 * 5` fires on every 13th and every
  Friday), AND-equivalent otherwise — a deliberate breaking change from the
  initial AND behavior, chosen to match user expectation and standard cron;
  a stepped day field (`*/2`) counts as restricted (documented divergence
  from Vixie's `*`-spelling flag so every valid expression provably fires
  within the bounded scan). Unsatisfiable expressions now fail validation
  instead of silently never firing
  ([LLP 0028](./0028-cron-scheduled-execution.rfc.md) §Calendar expression
  semantics).
- **2026-07-02:** the DK-4 gated leg ships: queries and mutations reuse a
  **warm VM per bundle** (keyed by exact module source, per app instance)
  behind a **verified per-invocation reset** — baseline-restore of
  `globalThis`, the dispatch table, and a fixed intrinsic-root list, with
  fail-closed burn-and-recreate on any doubt; actions and hosted/untrusted
  stay fresh; the posture is capability-flagged (`vmLifecycle`) and
  opt-out-able (`SNAPBACK_FRESH_VM=1`). Adversarial review then found and the
  same landing fixed a HIGH cross-invocation context-injection bug (a
  module-scope-stashed handler continuation firing during a later invocation
  would inject a cross-user write) with an **invocation-epoch
  stale-continuation guard** that rejects any host call whose originating
  invocation is not the live one — plus a broadened reset root-set and a pool
  generation counter. The gate held: the `dk4_*` leak suite is the proven
  reset, and includes a cross-invocation regression test that fails against
  the pre-guard code. Measured (LLP 0035 §After warm VM, two runs, the guard
  adds no measurable cost): warm invocation ~800–900 µs vs fresh ~3.6 ms
  (4.5–6.1×), single-writer ~4–5× to ~1 050–1 280 mut/s, fan-out ~2.4–3× to
  ~1.5–2 ms/affected sub — fan-out is now row-bound (the DK-3 revisit
  trigger's territory), not VM-bound. Honest limit recorded: module-level
  closure *state* persists across warm invocations (standard pooled-runtime
  semantics; cross-invocation *execution* is separately fenced by the epoch
  guard), pinned by a characterization test
  ([LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md) §DK-4 as-built).
- **2026-07-02:** the schema source of truth (docket DK-7) is decided and
  ratified by Charlie (decision drafted and applied by Claude under Charlie's
  same-day delegation): **the Rust types the server parses are the single
  source of truth** for every Snapback-owned shared artifact shape; JSON
  Schemas are generated from them and committed at `schemas/`, JS codegen and
  tools validate against the committed schemas, and a Rust snapshot test is
  the CI drift gate ([LLP 0037](./0037-schema-source-of-truth.decision.md)).
  The Exact-owned `ContractDataManifest` is explicitly out of scope — the
  obligation there is a validated mapping, not schema authority. Unblocks
  LLP 0032 P3(c)/P2 and answers the open questions LLP 0016 Phase 1,
  LLP 0020, and LLP 0024 blocked on.
- **2026-07-02:** the commit-version graduation shape (docket DK-2, LLP 0032
  X1) is decided and ratified by Charlie (drafted and applied by Claude under
  Charlie's same-day delegation): the portable contract is **per-app version
  streams** (LLP 0018's boundary), **gap-free in commit-visibility order**,
  with wire cursors as opaque `(stream, version)` pairs never compared across
  streams; allocator mechanics stay target-owned — SQLite's serialized commit
  as shipped, **commit fencing** as the Postgres graduation default (with the
  logical-decoding change feed recorded as the measured scale escape), and a
  per-object Durable Object counter with **one version authority per app**
  named as the Cloudflare Profile C constraint. HLC-style ordering is
  rejected for v1 with a named revisit trigger (multi-master writes within
  one app stream). Design note in
  [LLP 0007](./0007-storage-portability.spec.md) §Commit-version graduation;
  wire pin in [LLP 0012](./0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md)
  §Versioning ahead of LLP 0032 P4's protocol work; Cloudflare answer in
  [LLP 0014](./0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md) §Durable State.
- **2026-07-02:** the v1 auth/sync direction in
  [LLP 0011](./0011-auth-and-permissions.rfc.md) is ratified by Charlie
  (applied by Claude under Charlie's same-day delegation), answering that
  RFC's own "ratify before sync hardens" ask ahead of LLP 0032 P4:
  **server-directed resubscribe on permission change** — declared auth
  dependencies, authorization epochs advanced by permission-affecting
  writes, `resync_required` to affected subscriptions, fail-closed client
  stale handling, epoch-compared reconnect with forced resnapshot when stale
  — plus the **user-switch full wipe** of local data, queues, and
  subscriptions. Precise live `remove` deltas remain a later optimization
  for analyzable rules. The RFC stays Draft (the auth surface is unbuilt);
  the direction is what P4's protocol and LLP 0020's auth-aware matcher
  design against.
- **2026-07-02:** [LLP 0036](./0036-app-secrets-and-config.rfc.md) (app
  secrets and configuration) → **Accepted**, ratified by Charlie (applied by
  Claude under Charlie's same-day delegation): the v1 slice is built and
  verified on the local target (declared config + grants, the 0600
  sibling-file store, `ctx.secrets` handles, gated config ops with the
  transit rule, the setup wizard, and the All Aboard/G4 migration), one
  cross-family review round is applied, and the implementation-status banner
  keeps the honest gaps (host-mediated fetch/G3, probe timeouts, non-secret
  entries, hosted backends, CLI/MCP projections pending LLP 0032 P6) as the
  claim boundary.
- **2026-07-02:** the DK-3 revisit trigger *fired* (M3 re-measurement after
  DK-4, the trigger's own instruction). Warm-VM whole-query fan-out still caps
  live delivery at ~5–6 affected subscribers per commit within a 10 ms budget;
  the op-log-delta projection for the analyzable syncable-live subset (classify
  each changed row against the captured predicate with the live matcher, no VM,
  no re-query) is ~5 200× cheaper (~0.32–0.36 µs vs ~1 680–1 970 µs per affected
  sub), a ceiling of ~28 000+ subscribers/commit
  ([LLP 0035](./0035-local-target-benchmarks.research.md) §M3; the specific
  figures are superseded — the `37f1a2f` fixture corrections invalidated
  them, no corrected full-scale rerun exists, see LLP 0035's 2026-07-21
  note). Op-log deltas
  for the analyzable subset are the sanctioned **next fan-out lever**; the delta
  shape and server emission are Snapback's, while the wire kind and client apply
  are shared surface with the LLP 0034 provider-side kit and are sequenced with
  it (an additive `live_delta` beside the honest `live_replace` fallback, never
  a breaking change). The cacheable-HTTP read lane (LLP 0022) remains the
  deferred larger option behind its own hosted/multi-instance trigger. Recorded
  in [LLP 0008](./0008-local-first-sync-and-realtime.rfc.md) §Decision DK-3.
- **2026-07-02:** op-log deltas *landed* — the DK-3 lever shipped end to end as
  an additive `live_delta` wire kind beside `live_replace`, resolving
  [LLP 0034](./0034-contract-source-seam-provider-side.rfc.md)'s Open Question 1
  (row diffs). For the analyzable subset (an unordered single-table filtered
  **HostPlan** query — where the predicate *is* the query, so a matcher-derived
  delta is sound), live delivery now ships the per-row add/update/remove
  transitions instead of re-running the query; the server maintains membership
  by applying the ops (a debug-build convergence assertion guards it) and the
  kit applies them client-side. Ibex queries (which may transform results beyond
  their predicate) and ordered/limited windows keep the honest `live_replace`
  fallback, so nothing breaks and no client that only understands `live_replace`
  regresses. This closes DK-3's op-log-delta leg; the cacheable-HTTP read lane
  stays deferred.
- **2026-07-02 (pending Charlie's ratification nod):** host-plan mutation
  isolation (docket DK-1, leg E3) is decided — **Option A: the client-supplied
  `read_set` is the isolation mechanism for atomic host-plan mutations, by
  design.** A host-plan `MutationPlan` is an *unconditional* write sequence whose
  reads and writes execute in one commit transaction, so a server-captured read
  can never be stale relative to the commit — "capture reads server-side" (the
  original E3 target) is a no-op for conflict detection under this atomic model.
  The read that can be stale is the *client's* optimistic read, taken before the
  request against a snapshot the server never saw; the client declares that
  baseline and `execute_mutation` enforces it atomically inside the commit
  transaction (version-match CAS, the shape of HTTP `If-Match`). This is **not** a
  trust gap: an omitted or dishonest baseline weakens only that mutation's own
  isolation, never other writers' atomic, version-stamped commits. The asymmetry
  with Ibex handlers is *justified* — Ibex reads → awaits → commits across an
  async window, so the server performed the deciding read early and must capture
  it; host-plan has no such window. **Option B** (host-plan mutations carrying
  declarative conditional predicates the server evaluates atomically) is recorded
  as a future, additive extension, not a correctness prerequisite. Zero code
  change (`execute_mutation` already validates `input.read_set`); the leg is
  honest docs + proving tests (`e3_hostplan_conflict_uses_client_baseline`,
  `e3_hostplan_without_baseline_commits_against_changed_state`). With E2 and E4
  (predicate-granular) already landed, **DK-1 is fully resolved**
  ([LLP 0032](./0032-decision-and-implementation-hardening.plan.md) Phase 2;
  LLP 0003/0004/0007). *The integrator will surface E3 for Charlie's veto; it is
  not pre-ratified.*
- **2026-07-02:** adapter live-push architecture (docket DK-10) is decided
  and ratified by Charlie (applied by Claude under Charlie's same-day
  delegation): **the Contract adapter owns the live subscription** — proved
  in code by the LLP 0034 D1/C1 implementation (single-query surfaces
  subscribe when the bound descriptor is live; the multi-query mode binds
  each named query to its own boundary key and connection axis over one
  shared socket), with All Aboard's `store.mjs` workaround deleted and the
  separate live-provider-seam alternative dead. The LLP 0032 P5 close-out —
  todo-demo defaults out of the generic `@snapback/contract` package
  (binding facts as required options, app-owned optimistic reducers) and the
  cron-authored-change-reaches-a-Contract-surface proof
  `demos/all-aboard/tests/acceptance/contract-live.mjs`
  ([LLP 0029](./0029-all-aboard-gap-closure.plan.md) G2) — re-landed
  2026-07-03 adapted to the post-sync main (ENG-22680).
- **2026-07-02:** the JS package namespace is decided and ratified by Charlie
  (applied by Claude under Charlie's same-day delegation): **`@snapback/*`**
  (`@snapback/contract`, `@snapback/codegen`), private workspace packages
  under `packages/*` with a repo-root workspace (LLP 0032 P6).
  Registry claiming/publishing is deliberately deferred to the first
  publish; if the npm scope proves unavailable then, the decision reopens
  (recorded in [LLP 0021](./0021-contract-exact-integration-hardening.plan.md)).
  The P6 build itself — the repo-root workspace, the `snapback` CLI +
  gated host-ops endpoint serving the first-slice verbs with one
  `hostOpsResult` envelope, and `start --ready-file` writing the LLP 0030
  readiness descriptor — re-landed 2026-07-03 adapted to the post-sync
  main (ENG-22680).
- **2026-07-02:** V8 conformance-twin timing (docket DK-5, LLP 0032 X2) is
  decided and ratified by Charlie (applied by Claude under Charlie's
  same-day delegation): **defer the synthetic twin** — the fail-closed
  handler-surface gates already do the anti-hardening job, the second
  target (Cloudflare) is V8 by construction so X3 is the first real
  conformance exercise, a second native bring-up would double the repo's
  worst DX pain, and L6/L7 re-scoped the busy-loop risk. Four named revisit
  triggers (X3 start; hosted/untrusted work; observed engine divergence;
  the limits gap turning user-facing) in
  [LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md) §Decision DK-5.
- **2026-07-02:** the client local-store mandate (docket DK-9, LLP 0032 X4)
  is decided and ratified by Charlie (applied by Claude under Charlie's
  same-day delegation): **a store-agnostic client-store interface with
  SQLite as the reference implementation** — under server-authoritative
  query-result sync the client store is a result cache + cursor/epoch
  state + outbox, never a relational replica; nothing on the wire may
  require SQL client-side; capability reports name the physical store
  ([LLP 0008](./0008-local-first-sync-and-realtime.rfc.md) §Decision DK-9).
- **2026-07-07:** the V8 conformance-twin deferral (docket DK-5) is
  **re-decided** after its first two revisit triggers fired the same day
  (LLP 0040 starting X3; LLP 0047 beginning hosted-limits work), ratified by
  Charlie in-session (applied by Claude): **build the twin now, as a
  protocol consumer rather than a native embed** — the RPC-shaped
  handler↔host seam makes the twin the same workerd-hosted harness the
  Cloudflare proof deploys, so the second-native-bring-up cost the deferral
  refused no longer applies. The enduring Hermes-local engine posture
  (device parity with Exact, capability security by construction, footprint,
  spinup) is recorded in
  [LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md) §Decision DK-5; the
  build is [LLP 0048](./0048-v8-conformance-twin.rfc.md),
  prerequisite-shaped for
  [LLP 0040](./0040-cloudflare-narrow-proof.rfc.md)'s first deploy.
- **2026-07-07:** the Cloudflare durable-store question (LLP 0014 Profiles
  A/B, open since 2026-06-26) is decided and **ratified by Charlie:
  everything on Durable Objects.** The per-app data plane lives in the
  write-owning object's SQLite — one local transaction commits version,
  rows, and journal
  ([LLP 0040](./0040-cloudflare-narrow-proof.rfc.md) revised in step,
  profile `cloudflare-do-sqlite-v8`) — and the later platform control plane
  is a registry Durable Object with gateway caching rather than D1, which
  leaves the architecture entirely. Fallbacks are preserved as named revisit
  triggers (data plane: LLP 0040 §Target shape; control plane:
  [LLP 0014](./0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md) §Resolution).
- **2026-07-13:** the 2026-07-02 v1 auth/sync direction is **narrowly refined,
  not reversed**. Server-resolved authorization, fail-closed stale-result
  handling, user-switch full wipe, and a fresh authorized snapshot before live
  release remain ratified. [LLP 0053](./0053-authorization-invalidation.rfc.md)
  is now Accepted and is the controlling contract for the narrower invalidation
  mechanism: ordinary matcher-observed row changes do not all advance a coarse
  authorization epoch; result shrinkage is carried by witnessed delivery
  obligations; enumerated writeless/permission-backing events use the
  authorization version; every non-live auth-scoped resume fresh-snapshots;
  protected mutable inputs fail closed; ephemeral room membership reauthorizes;
  and retained reads use the bounded `AuthorizedLease`. The dated LLP 0008 and
  LLP 0011 amendments record the same precedence. ENG-24592's review gate is
  closed; acceptance removes that blocker without claiming implementation.
  Landing item 6 still follows item 5, and item 8 still follows items 5–7.
- **2026-07-13:** ENG-24596 implements auth-program item 3's local provider
  core under [LLP 0057](./0057-auth-provider-protocol.spec.md): the protected
  catalog and keyring, email-code challenges, session-token families,
  capture mailbox, lifecycle/GC, collision rescue, and quiesced physical
  backup/declared restore now have executable Rust authorities, generated DTO
  schemas, and adversarial tests. This does **not** activate sessions. Normal
  startup remains in `dev`; provider routes and operator session minting fail
  closed until item 4 installs the staged mode and shared invocation gate, and
  production sessions remain non-enableable until item 5 installs connection
  binding, revocation projection, and the `AuthCoordinator`. The non-production
  collision-rescue operator path remains reachable because it repairs the
  condition that can prevent ordinary startup.
- **2026-07-14:** the ordered auth-program implementation through ENG-24607 is
  now composed on the local target. LLP 0058's staged mode and shared invocation
  gate make server-derived handler authority the only sessions source; LLP
  0059's connection-bound projection, two-phase revocation, and
  `AuthCoordinator` close the activation fence; LLP 0053's accepted item-6/item-8
  contract supplies negotiated authorization invalidation and signed,
  equality-expiring retained-query leases; and the authority descriptor plus
  two-axis store separates principal-owned rows from credential-instance-owned
  queued work. The card-game proof exercises real finite/live separation,
  logout blanking, cold-process retained display, and expiry deletion. This is
  an implementation ledger entry, not an acceptance transition for Draft LLPs
  0057–0060. The local target remains explicitly not hosted-auth ready: it owns
  neither the public TLS edge, trusted effective-source derivation, shared
  throttling, multi-replica invalidation, nor sender-constrained browser
  credentials.
- **2026-07-15:** Charlie Cheever explicitly ratified LLPs 0057–0060 after
  ENG-25001 closed the material review findings. The final ratification-gate
  reviews were READY from both families: FINAL4 for LLPs 0057 and 0058,
  corrected FINAL7 Codex plus FINAL5 Fable for LLP 0059, and FINAL5 for LLP
  0060. All four documents are Accepted. This accepts LLP 0060's hosted
  deployment and client-page security contract; it does not claim the current
  local target implements the public hosted edge, and it does not advance
  frozen LLP 0039.
- **2026-08-02:** Charlie Cheever requested implementation of
  [LLP 0141](./0141-embeddable-rust-library.rfc.md), ratifying its deliberately
  narrow D1/D4/D5 decision set. Snapback now has one supported
  `snapback::embed` façade, a baked-artifact production builder that reuses the
  complete CLI startup lifecycle, and an explicit self-hosted-single-tenant
  native-code posture. This does not activate demand-gated route/native-op
  extension (D2/D3), claim crates.io or one-file distribution (D6), or weaken
  the hosted isolation and listener-posture boundaries.
- **2026-08-13:** Charlie Cheever ratified the **optimistic-landing default**:
  an ordinary source change whose affected tests passed and whose tested
  semantic delta survives non-semantic integration lands without repeating
  expensive verification merely because `main` advanced. Frozen-record drift
  gates targeting `origin/main` are selected and reported at pre-push but defer
  to the scheduled D-3-classified backstop by default; non-main refs claim no
  scheduled verdict. `SNAPBACK_DRIFT_GATES_STRICT=1` restores synchronous
  enforcement. This is a throughput/fix-forward ruling for ordinary landings,
  not a weakening of affected product tests or of publication, promotion,
  destructive migration, urgent-security, active-corruption, or comparable
  irreversible/high-danger protocols ([LLP 0232](./0232-green-pointer-development-model.rfc.md)
  §Optimistic landing default; [LLP 0189](./0189-verification-cadence-and-claim-gating.decision.md)
  D-2).

## Who Snapback is for

Snapback is for people building real applications who want a backend that gets
out of the way at the prototype stage and stays with them into production.

The first-class product context is Contract/Exact app development, especially
mobile apps. React and Expo are important secondary consumers, but they should
not drive core design when their needs conflict with Contract/Exact.

The canonical Exact-side references are the Exact repository's
[`README.md`](../../exact/README.md) and [`AGENTS.md`](../../exact/AGENTS.md),
plus the Exact LLPs that define Contract, Contract's default-authoring policy,
and Design Mode: [Exact 0216](../../exact/llp/0216-contract-explained.explainer.md),
[Exact 0160](../../exact/llp/0160-contract-by-default.rfc.md),
[Exact 0158](../../exact/llp/0158-design-mode.rfc.md), and
[Exact 0200](../../exact/llp/0200-contract-design-mode-authoring-platform.rfc.md).
For Snapback-specific Contract data binding context, see [Exact 0263](../../exact/llp/0263-contract-data-bindings-for-snapback-and-backends.rfc.md)
and [Exact 0265](../../exact/llp/0265-contract-snapback-data-authoring-ergonomics.rfc.md).
Snapback should cite those sources rather than redefining Contract/Exact. The
known Snapback-side integration boundary is drafted in
[LLP 0005](./0005-contract-exact-react-integration.explainer.md) and specified
in [LLP 0012](./0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md).

The first-class builder is an AI agent. Snapback's own implementation, generated
Snapback app code, and integration code should all assume agent authorship and
agent validation loops as the normal path. Human ergonomics are secondary to
giving agents strong affordances, tight feedback, and ways to get close to
guarantees.

A specific, important surface for that builder is Exact's **Design Mode** — where
an app is shaped by typing into a segment's chat box and turning knobs. Snapback
should integrate especially well with it. A useful design test for any Snapback
affordance: does a Design-Mode action (a sentence typed into a segment, a knob
turned) map cleanly onto the right backend behavior — the data read or written,
what syncs to the device, what updates live, who is allowed to see it — and is
that mapping legible enough for the agent to confirm it did the right thing? An
affordance that can't be driven well from Design Mode is a design smell.

The intended arc is one system across the whole lifecycle:

- **Prototype:** spin something up that costs ~nothing when idle and requires no
  infrastructure decisions.
- **Grow:** keep the same model and code as traffic increases.
- **Production:** carry a popular app without forcing a migration to a different
  stack. The north-star target is an app like Partiful reaching a few hundred
  thousand monthly active users on SQLite plus a lightweight JavaScript runtime
  (see [Scale targets and known limits](#scale-targets-and-known-limits)).

## Subsystems (v1)

These are the first-version building blocks. Each is sketched here at the
boundary level; the detailed design of each is expected to live in its own
follow-on LLP (see [Follow-on LLPs](#follow-on-llps)).

### Code execution

Backend logic runs as JavaScript. The runtime is pluggable:

- **Ibex** ([ccheever/ibex](https://github.com/ccheever/ibex)) for local
  deployments on your own machine and anywhere binary size matters.
- A **V8-based** runtime when running on Cloudflare, when you prefer it, or when
  you need its performance characteristics.

Both runtimes are *intended* to be interchangeable at the
programming-model level — the choice is meant to be an operational/target
decision, not a fork. That intent is **not yet earned**, but the mechanism that
earns it is now identified: a shared **runtime-conformance contract** (the
language/feature floor every acceptable engine must pass — see
[LLP 0009](./0009-code-execution-security.rfc.md) §Runtime Boundary), whose two
checkable properties are that the host can drive an `async` handler to
completion and does not hold the storage lock across async work. Valet's
`async`-handler rejection was an *execution-boundary* choice (synchronous eval
under the database lock), not an engine limit — Ibex already pumps the
microtask queue via `ex_hermes_poll` and Snapback adopts that boundary
([LLP 0003](./0003-local-code-hosting-and-storage-core.rfc.md) §Execution and
Write Semantics; [LLP 0006](./0006-valet-retrospective.research.md)). Until
that contract is implemented and verified, "one programming model" remains a
goal, not a property already in hand. Boa is not a Snapback engine option; only
Ibex and V8 are, unless a future, explicit reason names a third.

How executed code is isolated — the sandbox boundary around app code, and the
tenant-isolation model when one deployment serves multiple apps or is hosted for
other people — is a load-bearing constraint this explainer does not yet settle.
It is called out here and owned by a dedicated security follow-on (see
[Follow-on LLPs](#follow-on-llps)).

### Data storage

SQLite at the small scale, Postgres at the larger scale. The key constraint:
there is **one abstraction over both** that you use most of the time, so that
graduating from SQLite to Postgres is a mostly seamless transition rather than a
rewrite. Preserving that portability is a hard design requirement — anything
that makes the SQLite→Postgres move a rewrite has failed the brief.

The contract binds the shared abstraction: code written against it must move
from SQLite to Postgres without a rewrite. Reaching past it to engine-specific
features stays allowed — *paved path, not a cage* — but is an explicit opt-out
that trades away portability for that code. Where exactly the line sits, and
whether a portable subset is guaranteed, is for the storage LLP to pin down.

### Auth / login

For most apps, auth data is the most important data, and it is something teams
spend a lot of time setting up. Snapback treats it as first-class and keeps it
**next to the rest of your data**, so you can join it with your other tables,
query it, and export it freely. The paved path is a built-in system in the
spirit of WorkOS or Clerk; bringing your own provider (WorkOS, Clerk, or
anything else) is explicitly supported.

### Asset storage

Users want to upload photos and videos, so those are the priority, including a
**processing pipeline** for them. Other file types (PDFs, etc.) are supported
too, but photo/video processing drives the design. Asset bytes are normally
stored on the filesystem or in S3-style object storage as appropriate for where
you are hosting. Asset metadata, ownership, permissions, references, and
processing state live with the rest of the application data in SQLite/Postgres.

### Cron

Scheduled code execution. Every backend platform that matures grows this, so
Snapback includes it from the start.

### Notifications

Reach people by email, SMS, and push (and possibly other channels). This is a
**thin layer** that you configure with your own provider API keys, not a
reimplementation of those providers.

## Subsystems planned for v2 (post-launch)

These can wait until after the initial launch:

- **AI gateway** — call an AI API for something directly through Snapback.
- **Data gateway** — fetch data such as sports scores or weather through
  Snapback and pay for it where needed (sometimes free, sometimes paid).

## Cross-cutting requirements

These span every subsystem and are part of what makes Snapback distinct.

### Local-first with partial sync

Mobile apps must keep working offline, which means syncing the relevant slice of
data down to the device. The bar: a messaging app should still show your old
messages with no connection — the failure mode where WhatsApp would show *no*
messages offline is exactly what local-first exists to prevent. Full replication
is not assumed; **partial** sync of the data a given client needs is the model.

Desktop apps are an explicit case, not an afterthought, and they differ from
mobile in a way that matters here: far more data may live locally — a desktop
client might reasonably keep a large working set, or close to the whole dataset,
on disk. Partial sync therefore has to scale across a wide range of local-store
sizes, from a phone holding a thin slice to a desktop holding a great deal; the
sync model must not assume the mobile size profile.

### Realtime live updates

Changes should propagate to interested clients immediately. If someone RSVPs to
a party while another user is viewing the RSVP list, that guest should appear
under "Yes" and the count should increment without a refresh.

High-frequency transient state such as cursors, selections, typing indicators,
and online presence is realtime state too, but not ordinary durable data by
default. [LLP 0013](./0013-ephemeral-realtime-state.rfc.md) owns the proposed
ephemeral-state contract for that class: typed, TTL-bound latest-state
collections backed by process memory locally and adapter-specific infrastructure
on hosted targets.

### Multi-target deployment

Snapback should run on your own machine, your own hardware, Cloudflare, AWS,
Google Cloud, Azure, DigitalOcean, Hetzner, Railway, and Fly.io. **Cloudflare is
unusual** enough that making Snapback work well there is called out as requiring
dedicated effort rather than being assumed to come for free. A runtime/deployment
LLP must define target profiles and launch requirements before agents treat the
whole matrix as implemented. Cloudflare is important enough that doing it well is
the planned second step after the local core (see
[Implementation order](#implementation-order)), and we should be open to
rearchitecting around its model rather than treating it as a port.

This is also where *scale to zero* has to be made precise. Idle cost is
target-dependent: a serverless or Cloudflare-style substrate can bill to
(almost) zero when idle, while an always-on VM — a Hetzner box, a cloud droplet
— keeps incurring its base rate no matter how light the app is. "Costs almost
nothing when idle" is therefore a property the architecture must earn per
target, not a universal guarantee; the deployment LLP should state what idle
cost looks like for each target profile. **The canonical local target's
entry is measured** ([LLP 0035](./0035-local-target-benchmarks.research.md)
§Idle cost, LLP 0032 X5): a provisioned idle instance performs zero disk
writes and no measurable CPU over a 120 s window, at ~13 MiB resident —
idle means idle on the process the architecture controls; the
dollars-when-idle question remains each target profile's to state.

**The All Aboard Cloudflare proof's no-subscriber entry is measured too.**
After an operator reset cleared the durable subscription registry, five status
observations established the demand-gated warm poll at `inactive:no-demand`,
demand zero, and no next or last run; the final observation at 2026-07-11
23:32:44Z defines the disarm boundary. Settled script-attributed Adaptive
analytics across the two request datasets over the pure 7,200-second quiet
window 2026-07-11 23:37:44Z–2026-07-12 01:37:44Z observed no rows, which the
reader summarized as **0 estimated Worker requests, 0 estimated Worker execution GB-s,
and 0 estimated Durable Object requests**. The account-wide periodic query
likewise observed no row, summarized as 0 µs of Durable Object active time.
The adjacent pre- and post-window controls returned nonzero rows through the
same queries, and a post-window query returned 21 stations while the refresh
action succeeded with source `511-siri-stop-monitoring`, so absent quiet-window
rows are not explained by attribution lag or a dead deployment. Cloudflare
documents that Adaptive datasets may be sampled, and an empty quiet result
carries no sample interval or confidence field, so this is one controlled
observed-zero estimate, not an absolute event-absence or invoice guarantee.
The raw aggregate and hourly evidence is
checked in as
[`measurements.remote.idle.2026-07-12.json`](../targets/cloudflare/generated/measurements.remote.idle.2026-07-12.json).
The Durable Object periodic/storage datasets lack script identity and remain
account-wide context; storage had no positive control and its empty array is
telemetry absence, not evidence of zero stored bytes. The per-script Worker and
Durable Object request datasets carry the deployment-specific observation.

## Canonical local target

Of all the targets, one is privileged as the **standard place to build and
test**: the whole server — code hosting *and* data store — running **locally on
your machine via Ibex + SQLite**. Other configurations are valid (for example,
running only the Wasm part of the sync engine in the browser), but this local
Ibex + SQLite server is the **first standard target** — the default an agent
assumes when it builds, runs, and validates its work, and the baseline the
[reference apps](#reference-apps) and their acceptance suite run against. The
decision and its consequences are recorded in
[LLP 0002](./0002-canonical-local-target.decision.md).

## Reference apps

These are concrete apps Snapback must be able to build *and run well* — the
working set the design is validated against, not just demos. Each leans on a
different mix of the subsystems and cross-cutting requirements above; together
they set the capability bar. (Some lean on
[v2 subsystems](#subsystems-planned-for-v2-post-launch) and are correspondingly
post-launch targets.)

- **Todo list** — the floor: basic CRUD, auth, and offline edits on the
  scale-to-zero prototype path. If this isn't trivial, nothing else matters.
- **1-1 and group messaging** (Signal/WhatsApp) — local-first with partial sync
  plus realtime delivery; the canonical "show old messages with no connection"
  case from [Local-first with partial sync](#local-first-with-partial-sync).
- **Kanban board** — realtime collaborative edits, optimistic reordering, and
  modest fan-out.
- **E-commerce store** — write correctness (inventory, orders) and asset storage
  for product media; also pulls in payments, an integration this corpus does not
  yet cover.
- **Collaborative whiteboard** — shared cursors and edits propagated *keystroke
  by keystroke*: the extreme realtime fan-out case, and exactly the
  [open fan-out question](#scale-targets-and-known-limits) this corpus has not
  yet answered.
- **Collaborative rich-text pad** — a small focused multiplayer editor across
  platforms. This is a nice-to-have stress case distinct from the Notion-like
  workspace: it asks whether Snapback can support at least one good approach to
  rich-text collaboration — operation log, CRDT/OT bridge, block-level mutations,
  or another explicit design — with cursor/selection presence, offline replay,
  and realtime convergence. It does not require Snapback's ordinary row-sync
  model to solve every editor architecture automatically.
- **Task tracker** (Linear) — local-first partial sync over a large dataset, with
  a snappy offline UX as the bar.
- **Notion-like workspace** — documents-plus-databases with realtime collab and a
  large local dataset, especially on desktop: a stress test for local-first at
  scale (the [desktop](#local-first-with-partial-sync) large-local-data case) and
  for flexible, structured-and-freeform data.
- **Team chat / community hub** (a mini Slack or Discord) — channel-scale
  realtime fan-out, presence, history partial-sync, and notifications. A mini
  version is fine; we don't need every feature.
- **Social / video app** (TikTok/YouTube/Instagram/Twitter) — the
  [asset storage](#asset-storage) and processing pipeline at scale, plus
  read-heavy feed fan-out; the photo/video-first case that subsystem targets.
- **Blog** — read-heavy content with image assets; web is a secondary client
  target but should still be served well.
- **Multiplayer card game** — each player sees a different view (secret hands), so
  partial sync must be **authorization-aware** — syncing only what a viewer is
  permitted to see — over realtime, server-authoritative state.
- **Party app** (Partiful) — RSVP realtime and invite/reminder notifications;
  already the north-star [scale target](#scale-targets-and-known-limits).
- **Finance app** (Robinhood/Coinbase) — money-grade write correctness, realtime
  price reads, sensitive-data auth, and external market data (a **v2** data
  gateway consumer).
- **Daily-fantasy / fantasy-sports picker** — scheduled scoring and contest locks
  via Cron, realtime leaderboards, and external sports data (a **v2** data
  gateway consumer).

The aim is breadth of *kind*, not a feature-complete clone of each: together
they exercise every v1 subsystem and every cross-cutting requirement, and they
push the realtime, correctness, and asset axes to their edges. What "works well"
means for each is acceptance-suite work for a follow-on LLP.

## Non-goals

Setting expectations is part of the design. Snapback is **not** trying to be
excellent at the following, and we'd rather say so up front. (You may still be
able to build a small version of some of these — they just aren't what the system
is optimized for, and they must not steer the design.)

- **Fast-twitch realtime games** — massively-multiplayer first-person shooters
  and other fast-action multiplayer games that stream large volumes of
  positional state at high tick rates. These want authoritative low-latency game
  servers, not a SQLite-backed sync model.
- **Serverless mesh sync — apps that must work with no server, ever** (named
  2026-07-23, LLP 0086 CC-8 row 13). Offline-critical physical operations —
  aircraft cabins, restaurant floors, contested networks — where devices must
  keep syncing peer-to-peer with no server reachable are a real, paying
  segment, and it is the one place CRDT convergence is the only correct tool;
  Ditto serves it and pays the structural price (certificate-carried
  permissions over immutable fields, conflicts resolved at read time)
  ([LLP 0084](./0084-triplit-ditto.research.md) §Findings, §Synthesis).
  Snapback is server-authoritative by design: its offline story is client
  replicas and mutation replay against an authoritative backend, and its
  "local-first" language must never blur into a no-server mesh claim. This
  marker plays the same boundary-naming role as the fast-twitch-games bullet
  above and LLP 0001's SpacetimeDB and Nakama/Colyseus markers.
- **High-frequency write firehoses** — clickstream/ad-tech ingestion, large-scale
  IoT telemetry, or matching engines sustaining very high write rates. That
  throughput profile fits neither the small-scale stack nor the scale-up target.
- **Analytics / data-warehousing workloads** — heavy OLAP over huge datasets
  belongs in a warehouse (BigQuery, Snowflake, ClickHouse). Snapback's store is
  transactional and app-facing, not an analytics engine.
- **CDN-scale streaming infrastructure** — being the global live-video transcode
  and delivery backbone. Building an app that *hosts and plays* user video is in
  scope (see the social/video reference app); being a streaming CDN is not.
- **Heavy compute / ML training** — GPU training jobs and large batch-compute
  pipelines. Snapback runs app logic and should call out to dedicated services
  for these.

These are starting expectations, not a final boundary; the ratifying source can
prune or extend the list as the scale-up envelope is benchmarked.

## Design principles and non-obvious constraints

Future work is expected to honor these even when they are inconvenient.

- **Scale to zero, then scale up.** Lightness at idle is a primary design goal,
  not an afterthought. The same system must still scale up to a popular
  production app.
- **Comprehensive and coherent.** Prefer one roof where the pieces are designed
  together over a loose collection of services. The Rails comparison is
  intentional.
- **Paved path, not a cage.** Let people do whatever they want, but make the
  blessed ways of doing things work exceptionally well. Bring-your-own
  alternatives (e.g. external auth providers) should remain possible.
- **Agent-first developer experience.** We expect essentially all Snapback
  framework code, generated app backend code, and integration code to be written
  by AI agents. Agent DX is therefore the priority, and where it trades off
  against human ergonomics, agent DX wins. Concretely, the syntax, tooling, and
  APIs should give agents ways to get *close to guarantees* about what they
  intend, and should provide **complete feedback loops** so an agent can
  validate its own work and understand the current state of the system.
  (`ref-check`, run by both agents and CI, is an early example of this
  feedback-loop philosophy applied to the design corpus itself.)
- **Contract/Exact first; React/Expo a strong second.** The primary client
  target is the Contract/Exact app stack, and its needs drive core design
  decisions. React and Expo are first-class *secondary* consumers — supported as
  well as they can be, but not steering the design.
- **Truly open.** MIT licensed. No "funny business" source-available licenses
  that prevent hosting Snapback for other people.

## Scale targets and known limits

- **Target:** SQLite + Ibex should carry an app like Partiful to a few hundred
  thousand monthly active users. That is the goal the small-scale stack is being
  designed against, not yet a proven benchmark.
- **Needs definition:** the workload envelope must be specified before this
  becomes an engineering claim: concurrent users, reads/sec, writes/sec,
  realtime subscription fan-out, asset upload volume, cron load, notification
  throughput, latency targets, and acceptable degradation behavior.
- **Benchmark gate:** every reference-app family that is used to claim scale
  must have an acceptance profile with data shape, local-store size, mutation
  rate, subscription count, fan-out pattern, and degradation behavior. A demo
  proves product shape; a benchmark proves the scale claim. The runnable demo
  corpus and its Contract/Exact dependency policy are planned in
  [LLP 0015](./0015-demo-application-suite.plan.md).
- **Out of scope:** several workloads are explicit non-goals — fast-twitch
  realtime games, write firehoses, analytics warehouses, and more — collected under
  [Non-goals](#non-goals).
- **Open question:** the upper bound on high-frequency fan-out is not yet known
  — e.g. whether ~100 people on the same site, each with a continuously synced
  mouse position pushed out to every other client, is realistic on this stack.
  This needs empirical answers, ideally informed by what was learned from Valet.

## Lessons from Valet

Snapback's predecessor was **Valet** ([expo/valet](https://github.com/expo/valet)),
an earlier prototype whose general architecture remains directionally important:

- A small **Rust** service running alongside **SQLite**, executing your code and
  using SQLite for storage, is considered directionally correct.
- For the code engine, **Ibex** is fine, and **V8** should also work well.
- Empirical testing of Valet ran into real problems that made it hard. Those
  specific problems have now been recovered from Valet's experience diaries and
  LLP corpus in [LLP 0006](./0006-valet-retrospective.research.md), then
  promoted into the storage, sync, runtime, auth, DX, and integration LLPs. The
  recovered record is strong enough to constrain v1: no silent codegen drops, no
  handler source-only replay, no per-row version triggers for one logical
  mutation, no silent backpressure drops, no stale subscription initial state, no
  typecheck theater, and no pretending Ibex/Hermes can enforce hosted-code
  resource limits until it actually can. What remains unanswered is not the
  basic failure list but the production envelope: single-writer breaking point,
  fan-out ceiling, retention/matcher cost, hosted isolation, and idle-cost
  behavior by target.

## Implementation order

Direction, not a schedule. Build it in this order, with the first step treated as
a **narrow proof**, not as a compressed version of the whole product:

1. **Code hosting + data storage first, on Ibex + SQLite.** Get the core — your
   code running and your data stored — solid on the
   [canonical local target](#canonical-local-target) before broadening anything
   else. It is the foundation every other subsystem sits on. The first-slice
   architecture is split across
   [LLP 0003](./0003-local-code-hosting-and-storage-core.rfc.md) for the local
   Rust server and SQLite core, and
   [LLP 0004](./0004-application-data-api.rfc.md) for the application data API
   that apps and agents use.
2. **Then Cloudflare, done well.** Once the core feels good, the next move is to
   make Snapback work *well* on Cloudflare — which may mean more than a port.
   Cloudflare's model is unusual enough (see
   [Multi-target deployment](#multi-target-deployment)) that we should be open to
   **rearchitecting around how Cloudflare works** rather than bolting it onto a
   design shaped only by the local stack.

The **first implementation slice** is deliberately smaller than the v1 product
surface. It should prove:

- one trusted local app on Ibex + SQLite;
- generated schema and function artifacts loaded by the local server;
- one portable single-table indexed query shape;
- one server-authoritative mutation path with one atomic commit version;
- a named mutation isolation strategy, so reads performed before commit cannot
  silently violate invariants under concurrent writes;
- one syncable live-query subset with an authoritative initial snapshot and
  online realtime deltas;
- deterministic reset/seed and machine-readable diagnostics;
- a headless or React fixture that consumes the generated contract without
  Contract/Exact-private backend semantics.

That first slice should **not** claim offline mutation replay, hosted/untrusted
execution, Postgres graduation, Cloudflare, real auth providers, assets, cron,
notifications, rich text, high-frequency whiteboard fan-out, finance-grade money
semantics, or Design Mode authoring. Those later capabilities must appear as
explicit capability gaps, not as half-implemented hidden behavior.

Cloudflare remains the planned second target, but it needs its own strategy LLP
before the local core hardens around unportable assumptions. At minimum that
strategy should identify the target seams for connection ownership, change
notification, durable state, scheduler/alarm behavior, WebSocket/session
lifetime, runtime isolation, secrets, asset storage, and storage-engine choice.
That strategy is drafted as
[LLP 0014](./0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md).

Everything else — the rest of the deployment matrix, the v2 subsystems, and the
broader reference-app set — follows once the narrow local proof and the Cloudflare
strategy are in hand.

written) for at least:

- The **local code-hosting and storage core** — the first Rust companion server

- **Schema migrations and evolution**, and how they interact with the
  SQLite↔Postgres portability contract.
- **Observability and backend error tracking** — logs, health metrics, and
  grouped issue triage for *running* apps: the runtime feedback loop that
  agent-first DX implies, distinct from `ref-check`, which serves the design
  corpus. The local Phase-1 kernel is specified by
  [`0154-minimal-observability.rfc.md`](./0154-minimal-observability.rfc.md)
  and taught in the
  [Backend observability guide](../guide/operating/observability.md). Local
  Phase E0/E1 classification, grouping, and triage are specified by
  [`0156-minimal-error-tracking.rfc.md`](./0156-minimal-error-tracking.rfc.md)
  and taught in the
  [Backend error-tracking guide](../guide/operating/error-tracking.md).
  Hosted arms, declared product analytics, E2 alert delivery, and E3 client
  crash ingest remain deferred and unbuilt.
- A **reference-apps acceptance suite** — what "works well" means for each app in
  [Reference apps](#reference-apps), expressed as agent-checkable validation.
  Drafted as
  [`0010-agent-dx-and-acceptance.spec.md`](./0010-agent-dx-and-acceptance.spec.md).
- A **demo application suite** — the runnable demo/reference app corpus,
  beginning with todo, including shared Snapback backends, Contract/Exact and
  React surfaces, and the external Exact dependency policy. Drafted as
  [`0015-demo-application-suite.plan.md`](./0015-demo-application-suite.plan.md).
- A **diary-driven change program** — the priority order and ownership split for
  turning demo diary findings into product changes across Snapback core,
  adapters, and Exact/Contract. Drafted as
  [`0016-diary-driven-change-program.plan.md`](./0016-diary-driven-change-program.plan.md).
- A **seven-backend, five-app bakeoff** — the staged calibration, remediation,
  deep-app comparison, holistic ranking, and Snapback improvement synthesis
  across Snapback, Convex, Supabase, Firebase, InstantDB, SpacetimeDB, and
  Appwrite. Drafted as
  [`0102-seven-backend-five-app-bakeoff.plan.md`](./0102-seven-backend-five-app-bakeoff.plan.md).
- A **Convex-parity course-corrections program** — the tranche-sequenced plan
  that turns the Diary 0039 current-main rerun (55/45 for Convex) into
  gap-closure work: external package/runtime qualification, structured inputs
  and helper composition, relational/scheduling primitives, live and broader
  portable search, and guide/review polish — without regressing Snapback's
  evidence and ephemeral-state differentiators. Drafted as
  [`0115-convex-parity-course-corrections.plan.md`](./0115-convex-parity-course-corrections.plan.md).
- A **React-web improvement sweep and deferred backend championship** — six
  substantial application contracts built Snapback-first as an external npm
  consumer to surface improvement findings against frozen LLP 0102/0115
  competitor evidence and demand-driven probes, with Expo and Exact Native
  sweeps beside it; the symmetric Snapback/Convex/Supabase/Firebase ranking is
  preserved as a deferred phase behind a dry-well trigger. Drafted as
  [`0137-react-web-backend-championship.plan.md`](./0137-react-web-backend-championship.plan.md).
- **Declared application refusal codes** — the option space over the landed
  LLP 0108 refusal channel: registry-and-schema declaration surfaces,
  payload contracts in the LLP 0131 grammar, typed rejection unions
  (LLP 0115 CV-16), conformance-fixture and wire treatment, disposition
  participation, action-boundary composition, and migration. Drafted as
  [`0144-declared-app-refusal-codes.rfc.md`](./0144-declared-app-refusal-codes.rfc.md).
- An **Expo-hosted, Cloudflare-backed Snapback product** — first-party Expo
  identity, project ownership, billing, and product UX around WfP-isolated
  handler code and per-environment Durable Object data, with its commercial
  contract and gated cross-repository delivery program. Drafted as
  [LLP 0103](./0103-expo-hosted-snapback.rfc.md),
  [LLP 0104](./0104-expo-hosted-metering-and-commercial-contract.spec.md), and
  [LLP 0105](./0105-expo-hosted-snapback-delivery.plan.md).
- An **agent-native Expo account bootstrap** — immediate expiring workspaces
  for unknown agents, trusted-host in-agent signup, one-link claim and payment,
  duplicate-safe recovery, first-class robot credentials, and bounded
  unattended delegation. Drafted as
  [`0106-agent-native-expo-account-bootstrap.rfc.md`](./0106-agent-native-expo-account-bootstrap.rfc.md).
- A **decision and implementation hardening program** — the review-driven
  program that reconciles corpus statuses with reality and hardens the
  implementation to the ratified semantics, with defect/decision/test-debt
  registers. Drafted as
  [`0032-decision-and-implementation-hardening.plan.md`](./0032-decision-and-implementation-hardening.plan.md).
- **Exact Design Mode integration** — how Snapback affordances surface in, and are
  driven from, Exact's Design Mode. Drafted as
  [`0005-contract-exact-react-integration.explainer.md`](./0005-contract-exact-react-integration.explainer.md);
  the implemented Contract update-economy and reactive host-state model is
  [`0052-contract-update-economy-and-host-state.rfc.md`](./0052-contract-update-economy-and-host-state.rfc.md).
- A **headless integration contract** for Contract/Exact, React/Expo, tests, and
  agent tools. Drafted as
  [`0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md`](./0012-headless-integration-contract.spec.md).
- An **agent operating surface** for host operations, CLI/HTTP/MCP projections,
  and generated app-data MCP tools. Drafted as
  [`0024-agent-operating-surface.spec.md`](./0024-agent-operating-surface.spec.md);
  the first MCP implementation slice is
  [`0042-agent-mcp-surface.rfc.md`](./0042-agent-mcp-surface.rfc.md).
- **Agent-native application consumption** — the research and recommended
  build sequence for joining operation descriptors, context-bounded discovery,
  effective authority views, durable outcome reconciliation, preflight/commit
  continuity, and resumable consumer sessions without creating another API
  dialect. Drafted as
  [`0149-agent-application-consumption.research.md`](./0149-agent-application-consumption.research.md).
- A **Cloudflare strategy** — how to do Snapback well on Cloudflare, including
  whether to rearchitect around its model. Drafted as
  [`0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md`](./0014-cloudflare-strategy.rfc.md).

This list is a pointer to expected work, not a commitment to numbering or
sequence; create each via the `llp-create` workflow when its design is ready.

## Repository and LLP setup

This repository keeps its LLP corpus directly in [`llp/`](../llp), rather than
using an LLP submodule for the documents themselves. The agent-facing rules are
in [`AGENTS.md`](../AGENTS.md), validation is via [`ref-check`](../ref-check)
(also run in CI), and the project skill aggregate lives under
[`skills/`](../skills/).

The skill files in `skills/` are symlinks into branch-tracking upstream
submodules under [`vendor/llp`](../vendor/llp) and
[`vendor/ccheever-skills`](../vendor/ccheever-skills), then exposed to each
agent through that agent's project-local skill directory. Run
[`scripts/sync-agent-skills`](../scripts/sync-agent-skills) after updating the
upstream skill repos. The LLP workflow itself is maintained at
<https://github.com/ccheever/llp>.

+## Snapback 4 affected selection (2026-10-24)
+
+Author direction: Workstream FAST, Charlie Cheever, 2026-10-24. This implements
+D-2 for the independent Snapback 4 workspace. It does not amend D-1, D-6, LLP
+0232's optimistic integration rule, or any publication protocol.
+
+`node snapback4/scripts/affected.mjs --base REV [--head REV | --worktree]
+[--json] [--run]` selects the merge-base-to-head Git diff. Worktree mode adds
+staged, unstaged, deleted, rename endpoints and non-ignored untracked paths.
+Historical planning reads the selected revision's manifests and test sources;
+execution requires that revision checked out. The root local-proof policy
+selects a `snapback4-affected` delegation gate for `snapback4/` paths. This
+independent workspace does not inherit the legacy Exact/admin baseline;
+other root groups and root unknown-path fallback retain it.
+
+Selection ownership:
+
+- Cargo metadata supplies workspace membership, optional/dev dependency edges
+  and integration targets. Source changes select the package and its transitive
+  reverse dependencies. A test-only file selects its exact integration target;
+  shared/nested support widens instead. Required target features are included.
+- Package manifests supply client dependency edges, including peer/dev edges.
+  Source changes select the package's test pool and its reverse dependencies;
+  a test-only diff selects named files. Tests' literal imports/re-exports,
+  dynamic literal imports, resource URLs and helpers are followed to find CLI
+  process and wasm consumers. Unresolved local/computed imports conservatively
+  count as both consumers. Tests spawning processes are conservatively CLI
+  consumers. Selected clients rebuild the products they consume. Existing
+  process-custody pooling and serial model custody are reused.
+- Guide/docs select check-guide, docs budget and packaged-docs lint; Snapback 4
+  guide cards also select the compiled guide target. Rust source movement
+  conservatively selects diagnostics-registry checking and check-inventories,
+  even without a new code. Language/generated inputs select generated-artifact
+  checking; affected references and LLPs select ref-check. Device reverse
+  dependents select wasm measurement. Language/core semantics select Lean
+  build, difftest run-lean and a seeded 2,000-case run.
+- Unknown paths, locks, workspace/package manifests, toolchains, build scripts,
+  proc-macros, shared test support and vendored inputs select all workspace
+  packages, client pools and cross-cutting checks. Each triggering file has a
+  reason. A package with no owned test runner also widens, covering tests held
+  in sibling packages. Selector implementation/fixture edits select their own
+  tests rather than product pools. Unowned root paths remain broad.
+
+Every execution command has a finite timeout and inherits
+`CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0 CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_DEBUG=0 CARGO_PROFILE_TEST_DEBUG=0`.
+Cargo disables target-level fail-fast; independent commands continue after
+failures, including producer failures (which remain visible). A JSON receipt
+under `target/affected/` records base/head, changed paths, selected commands,
+per-file reasons and per-command status/timing. It records incomplete execution
+before starting and refuses success if the observed Git/content snapshot changes.
+An affected receipt is landing evidence, never a packed-publication receipt.
+
+A historical red HEAD does not mean its last commit introduced that red. The
+maintained-column regression at `cadb7c55be` must be selected from the range
+starting before its originating change `202826334f`; selecting only the
+last documentation commit cannot discover inherited product failures. The
+regression fixture requires core/lang packages and residency.test.mjs for that
+originating range. Scheduled full-battery results remain the failure oracle;
+selection replay alone is not execution or a no-missed-failures proof.

+# @ref LLP 0189#snapback-4-affected-selection-2026-10-24 — ordinary landings are affected.
+snapback4_affected() {
+    if [ -n "$PROOF_SELECTION_RECEIPT" ]; then
+        node snapback4/scripts/affected.mjs --proof-receipt "$PROOF_SELECTION_RECEIPT" --run
+    else
+        node snapback4/scripts/affected.mjs --complete --run
+    fi
